9    Global optimality of the BCC lattice for p near 2
In this section we use a perturbation argument to extend the global optimality result of Barnes
and Sloane (1983) [11] from the case p = 2 to the case p near 2.

                                                   85
    Recall that the Selling parameters associated to a lattice basis A ∈ GL(3, R) are the six
real numbers ρ01 , ρ02 , ρ03 , ρ12 , ρ13 , ρ23 defined by
                                                                                
                            ρ01 + ρ12 + ρ13           −ρ12            −ρ13
                AT A =            −ρ12          ρ02 + ρ12 + ρ23      −ρ23       .
                                   −ρ13               −ρ23       ρ03 + ρ13 + ρ23

See for example [11]. If A has columns t1 , t2 , t3 and t0 := −t1 − t2 − t3 , then ρij = −ti · tj ,
i, j ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3}, i < j. Also, |t1 |2 = ρ01 +ρ12 +ρ13 , |t2 |2 = ρ02 +ρ12 +ρ23 , |t3 |2 = ρ03 +ρ13 +ρ23 .
The Selling parameters are invariant under rotations and reflections of the lattice but not
under dilations and change of basis, i.e., A and QA have the same Selling parameters for any
Q ∈ O(3), but if λ > 0 and U ∈ GL(3, Z), then A and λAU do not have the same Selling
parameters in general.
     We will use the Selling parameters and a result of Voronoi to restrict the domain a of Gp
to a set with a unique representative of the BCC lattice and at least one representative of
every lattice.

Definition 9.1 (Definition of aosb ). Given ρ = (ρ01 , ρ02 , ρ03 , ρ12 , ρ13 , ρ23 ) ∈ R6 , define the
symmetric matrix M (ρ) by
                                                                                  
                        ρ01 + ρ12 + ρ13        −ρ12                 −ρ13
              M (ρ) =       −ρ12         ρ02 + ρ12 + ρ23           −ρ23           .
                             −ρ13              −ρ23          ρ03 + ρ13 + ρ23

If det(M (ρ)) > 0, let T (ρ) be the unique upper triangular matrix such that M (ρ) has Cholesky
factorisation M (ρ) = T (ρ)T T (ρ), which is defined by
                             √
                                 M11 pM21 /T11              M31 /T11
                                                                           
                                                  2   (M
                    T (ρ) =  0           M22 − T12    p 32 − T13 T12 )/T22 .
                                                                           
                                  0          0                    2
                                                         M33 − T13 − T23 2


By construction T (ρ) has Selling parameters ρ. Let S be the following restricted set of
nonnegative Selling parameters:

            S = ρ ∈ [0, 1]6 : det(M (ρ)) > 0, M (ρ)33 ≤ M (ρ)22 ≤ M (ρ)11 = 1
                 


and let Aosb be the following set of lattice basis matrices:

                                        Aosb = {T (ρ) : ρ ∈ S} .

By construction, the elements of Aosb are upper triangular with first column equal to (1, 0, 0)T .
Finally, let aosb ⊂ R5 be defined by

                                            aosb = â(Aosb ),
where the map â was defined in equation (6.6).

   The subscript osb in aosb and Aosb is short for obtuse superbase. The reason for this will
be explained in the proof of the following lemma.

Lemma 9.2 (Properties of aosb ). The set aosb satisfies the following:

                                                    86
  (i) aosb ⊂ a;

 (ii) inf a∈a Gp (a) = inf a∈aosb Gp (a);

(iii) there exists a unique representative of the BCC lattice in aosb ; to be precise
                                                                √         √ √ !
                                                            1  2  2   1     2 2
                        â T 31 , 31 , 13 , 13 , 13 , 13 = − ,
                                                        
                                                                    ,− ,−    ,√
                                                            3 3       3    3    3

      is the unique representative of the BCC lattice in aosb , in the sense that it is the only
      element a ∈ aosb such that Λ(Â(a)) = Λ(λQAbcc ) for some λ > 0, Q ∈ O(3);

 (iv) volume upper bound: |V (Â(a))| ≤ 1 for all a ∈ aosb ;

  (v) diameter lower bound: diam V (Â(a)) ≥ 1 for all a ∈ aosb .
                                            

Proof. (i) Let a = (a1 , a2 , a3 , a4 , a5 ) ∈ aosb . Then a = â (T (ρ)) for some ρ ∈ S. Let
A = T (ρ) and let t1 = (1, 0, 0)T , t2 , t3 denote the columns of A. Since AT A = M (ρ), we
have |t1 |2 = M (ρ)11 = 1, |t2 |2 = M (ρ)22 , |t3 |2 = M (ρ)33 . Therefore, by definition of S,
1 = |t1 |2 ≥ |t2 |2 ≥ |t3 |2 and hence 1 ≥ a21 + a22 ≥ a23 + a24 + a25 . Note that det(M (ρ)) =
det(T (ρ))2 = (a2 a5 )2 . By definition of T (ρ), a2 = T (ρ)22 ≥ 0 and a5 = T (ρ)33 ≥ 0. Therefore
the assumption det(M (ρ)) > 0 ensures that a2 , a5 > 0. Hence a ∈ a.
   (ii) It is sufficient to show that

                              {Gp (a) : a ∈ aosb } = {Gp (a) : a ∈ a}.

Let a ∈ a and A = Â(a). By a theorem of Voronoi [38, Theorem 8], the lattice Λ(A)
has an obtuse superbase, which means that there exists Aosb ∈ GL(3, R) with the following
properties: Λ(Aosb ) = Λ(A) and, if t1 , t2 , t3 are the columns of Aosb and t0 := −t1 − t2 − t3 ,
then ti · tj ≤ 0 for all i, j ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3}, i ̸= j. Therefore the Selling parameters ρij := −ti · tj
of Aosb are nonnegative. Without loss of generality we can assume that |t1 |2 ≥ |t2 |2 ≥ |t23 |.
(If not, then we just reorder the columns of Aosb , which does not change the lattice Λ(Aosb );
it simply permutes the indices of the Selling parameters.)
     Since Aosb and T (ρ) have the same Gram matrix, there exists Q ∈ O(3) such that Aosb =
QT (ρ). Define
             √    λ = ρ01 + ρ12 + ρ13 , ρ̃ =√ρ/λ ∈ S, B = T (ρ̃), b = â(B) ∈ aosb . Then
B = T (ρ)/ λ. Therefore Aosb = QT (ρ) = λQB and
                                                          √            √        
                   Λ(Â(a)) = Λ(A) = Λ(Aosb ) = Λ λQB = Λ λQÂ(b) .

Since Gp is invariant under orthogonal transformations and dilations of the lattice, we have
Gp (a) = Gp (b) with b ∈ aosb , as required.
    (iii) Up to a dilation, the unique way of representing the BCC lattice with nonnegative
Selling parameters is ρλ = λ(1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1), where λ > 0 is a dilation [11]. (Note that there
is not a unique representation if you drop the nonnegativity condition, e.g., the BCC lattice
generated by abcc has Selling parameters ρ = (3/2, 3/2, 7/4, 0, −1/2, −1/2).) The only choice
of λ such that ρλ ∈ S is λ = 1/3 (this ensures that M (ρ)11 = 1). Therefore â(T (ρ1/3 )) is the
only representative of the BCC lattice in aosb , as required.
    (iv) Let a ∈ aosb . By (i), a ∈ a, which implies that a2 ∈ (0, 1] and a5 ∈ (0, 1]. Therefore
|V (Â(a))| = a2 a5 ≤ 1.


                                                 87
    (v) Let a = â (T (ρ))) ∈ aosb and A = Â(a) = T (ρ). Let t1 = (1, 0, 0)T , t2 , t3 denote the
columns of A and define t0 = −t1 − t2 − t3 . By the proof of (iii), the set {t0 , t1 , t2 , t3 } is an
obtuse superbase for the lattice Λ(A). Consequently ±t1 are Voronoi vectors for the lattice
[38, Theorem 3], which means that the following two planes intersect the boundary of the
Voronoi cell V (A):

                       ±{x ∈ R3 : x · t1 = |t1 |2 /2} = ±{x ∈ R3 : x1 = 1/2}.

In particular, diam(V (A)) ≥ 1, as required.

Proposition 9.3 (Existence of minimizers of Gp ). For all p ∈ [1, ∞), there exists a ∈ aosb
such that Gp (a) = inf aosb Gp .

A     Derivation of Ep from the quantization problem
In this appendix we give a rigorous derivation of the geometric optimization problem (1.3)
from the quantization problem (2.2) using the concept of Γ-convergence, which is recalled in
Section A.1. We also use Γ-convergence in Section 9 to prove the global optimality of the
BCC lattice for p near 2.

A.1     Facts from the calculus of variations
Let (X, d) be a metric space and let Φε : X → R, ε > 0. We recall that Φε Γ-converges to Φ0
                           Γ
as ε → 0, and write Φε → Φ0 , if the following are satisfied:

  (i) The liminf inequality: For all sequences xε → x,

                                          Φ0 (x) ≤ lim inf Φε (xε ).
                                                        ε→0


 (ii) The limsup inequality: For all x ∈ X, there exists a recovery sequence xε → x such that

                                          Φ0 (x) ≥ lim sup Φε (xε ).
                                                        ε→0




                                                   91
See, e.g., [41]. The definition of Γ-convergence may appear unnatural compared to the def-
inition of pointwise convergence of functions, but it has the following important property:
                   Γ
Suppose that Φε → Φ0 , that xε is global minimiser of Φε , and xε → x0 ∈ X. Then x0 is a
global minimiser of Φ0 . In other words, global minimisers of Φε converge to global minimisers
of Φ0 . This need not hold if Φε converges pointwise to Φ0 , but it is an immediate consequence
of the definition of Γ-convergence: Take any y ∈ X and let yε → y be a recovery sequence.
Then
                         (i)                                                                       (ii)
               Φ0 (x0 ) ≤ lim inf Φε (xε ) ≤ lim sup Φε (xε ) ≤ lim sup Φε (yε ) ≤ Φ0 (y).
                               ε→0                     ε→0                    ε→0

Therefore x0 is a minimiser of Φ0 . Moveover, taking y = x0 gives limε→0 min Φε = min Φ0 .
    We will prove that Ep arises as the Γ-limit of the lattice quantization error (Theorem A.1),
which justifies our choice of studying minimisers of Ep as a way of understanding solutions
of the quantization problem.

A.2      Facts from optimal transport theory
For p ∈ [1, ∞), the p-Wasserstein distance Wp (ρ, ν) between an absolutely continuous prob-
ability measure ρ ∈ L1 (Ω; [0, ∞)), Ω ρ dx = 1, and a discrete measure ν = N

More generally, Wp (µ1 , µ2 ) can be defined for any µ1 , µ2 ∈ P(Ω), and Wp : P(Ω) × P(Ω) →
[0, ∞) is a metric on P(Ω) [92, 98], but the special case (A.1) of semi-discrete optimal transport
is sufficient for our purposes (semi-discrete since ρ is absolutely continuous and ν is discrete).
    We recall the proof of Lemma 2.2 of the equivalence of the two formulations of the quanti-
zation problem, not only to make this paper more self-contained, but also because some steps
of the proof are important for Theorem A.1.

A.3    Γ-convergence of the quantization error to Ep
The following theorem is our main motivation for studying the optimization problem (1.3).
Theorem A.1. Let Ω ⊂ R3 be open and bounded, |Ω| = 1, and ∂Ω = φ(K) for some
Lipschitz continuous function φ : R2 → R3 and some compact set K ⊂ R2 . Given ε > 0 and
an invertible matrix A ∈ GL(3, R), define Λε (A) to be the rescaled lattice Λε (A) := Λ(εA).
Define Zε (A) to be the set of lattice points that lie in Ω, Zε (A) = Λε (A) ∩ Ω, and {Vz }z∈Zε (A)
to be the Voronoi tessellation of Ω generated by Zε (A):

                         Vz = {x ∈ Ω : |x − z| ≤ |x − w| ∀ w ∈ Zε (A)}.

Proof of (A.12): Let x ∈ Ω \ Ωε,A , x ∈ Vz for some z ∈ Zε (A) \ Yε (A). Take any z̃ ∈ Λε (A)
such that |x − z̃| = dist(x, Λε (A)); in other words z̃ is a closest lattice point to x in the
unrestricted lattice. If z̃ ̸= z, then the closest lattice point to x lies outside Ω and so
dist(x, ∂Ω) ≤ |x − z̃| ≤ 12 ε diam(V (A)). Otherwise we have z̃ = z. We split this case into
two subcases. First suppose that Vz ⊂ εV (A) + z. Then at least one point of εV (A) + z lies
outside Ω. Therefore dist(x, ∂Ω) ≤ ε diam(V (A)). Now suppose that Vz ̸⊂ εV (A) + z. We
define the set of neighbours of z by

                     Nz = z ′ ∈ Λε (A) : (εV (A) + z) ∩ (εV (A) + z ′ ) ̸= ∅ .
                           

In other words, z and z ′ are neighbours if their Voronoi cells are neighbours in the Voronoi
tessellation of R3 generated by Λε (A). There exists z ′ ∈ Nz such that z ′ ∈
                                                                            / Ω, otherwise we
would have Vz ⊂ εV (A) + z. Therefore

        dist(x, ∂Ω) ≤ |x − z ′ | ≤ |x − z| + |z − z ′ | ≤ 21 ε diam(V (A)) + ε diam(V (A)).

Therefore δε = 32 ε diam(V (A)). This proves (A.12).
   By (A.11) and (A.12), for any η > 0, if ε is sufficiently small then

                  |Ω \ Ωε,A | ≤ |Bδε (∂Ω)| ≤ 2δε (H2 (∂Ω) + 2η) → 0 as ε → 0.

This proves (A.10). We have

                                 1 = |Ω| = |Ω \ Ωε,A | + Mε (A)ε3 |V (A)|.                                     (A.13)

Combining (A.10) and (A.13) gives (A.9) and completes the proof of the limsup inequality.
  Next we prove the liminf inequality. Take any A ∈ GL(3, R) and any sequence Aε → A.
We need to show that
